How the exam works
  • Closed notes, no devices.
  • Name your method in the first line: “By induction”, “By contraposition”, “Assume for a contradiction”. Label base case and induction step.
  • Know cold: truth tables, English ↔ logic, negating quantifiers, and the definitions of odd, even, rational, irrational and |x|. Rule-of-inference names aren't tested.
  • About a quarter of problems come from the homework, often word for word: redo HW 5 and HW 6 cold.
  • Review materials will be posted on bCourses; do them when they appear.

Readiness check

Loading your progress…

Pass = 4/5 on a section's quiz (plus its proof task); that unlocks the next section. Mastered = right again in two separate recall rounds. A miss in recall or on a practice midterm flags it for review.

Recall rounds

Memorize trainer

Flashcards for the sections you've passed and the one you're on; more appear as you unlock sections. Knew it pushes a card later (10 min → 1 h → 6 h → 1 day); Missed it brings it back soon. Keys: space flip, 1 2 3 rate.

Study plan & daily pace

Daily pace

Every section is passed by Monday night and all three practice midterms are done by Wednesday midday, which leaves Wednesday afternoon free before Thursday's exam. The bar at the top shows today's target and whether you're ahead.

Day by day

This assumes you're seeing everything for the first time and covers every section. That's about 6 hours a day,. For every section: read the gist and goals → read the section → do the warm-up → do 2–3 textbook exercises → pass the mastery quiz, which unlocks the next section. Start and end every day with a recall round (~10 min each); that's where sections become mastered and where forgotten ones get caught.

Adjusting the plan
  • Behind schedule? Use that day's flex block first. Still behind? Cut in this order: Practice C (do only your weak-topic problems), the Tuesday extra problems, Saturday's §9 games and §13 sidebars. Never cut: Monday's new material, Practice A and B, the memorize sheet.
  • Ahead? Do more textbook exercises in the sections with the fewest ticks, or start Practice A on Monday night.
  • Scored under 70% on a practice topic? That section goes first in the next flex block: reread its gist and examples, redo its checks, then do one textbook exercise.
  • Scored 85%+ on a test? Turn the next fix block into extra flex time.
  • Every night: look at the readiness check. Anything listed is tomorrow's first task.

Course overview
PartWhat it's aboutPicture it
I · LogicHow mathematical statements are built (and, or, if-then, for all, there exists) and how to negate them.The grammar you need before you can write an essay.
II · ProofsHow to argue that a statement is always true: direct, contraposition, contradiction, cases, examples and counterexamples.A lawyer's case: every step must follow from evidence or earlier steps.
III · Sets & functionsThe objects math talks about (collections and rules that match inputs to outputs) and how to compare infinite sizes.Bags of items, and pairing items up one to one.
IV · Number theoryDivisibility, remainders, primes, gcd, solving equations mod m, and how it all powers RSA encryption.Clock arithmetic: numbers that wrap around.
V · InductionProving infinitely many cases at once, defining things step by step, recursive algorithms.Dominoes: knock over the first one, and each one knocks over the next.
VI · CountingCounting outcomes without listing them: multiply, divide out overcounts, pigeonhole, permutations, combinations.Ordering from a menu: an appetizer and a main multiplies; soup or salad adds.
VII · ProbabilityProving identities by counting two ways, the binomial theorem, stars and bars, and probability as “counting with a denominator.”Two people counting the same crowd from different spots must get the same number.

Part I

Logic

§1Propositions & connectives

Lecture 1 · Rosen §1.1 · HW 1.1–1.3

A proposition is a sentence that's either true or false. The connectives (not, and, or, if-then, iff) glue propositions together, and each one is defined by its truth table.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Decide if a sentence is a proposition
  • Build a truth table for any formula
  • Translate English with “only if,” “unless,” “necessary,” “sufficient”
  • Write the converse, contrapositive and inverse
Memorize
  • Truth tables of ¬, ∧, ∨, ⊕, →, ↔
  • p → q is false only when p = T, q = F
  • “p only if q” = p → q; “p unless q” = ¬q → p
  • Precedence: ¬, ∧, ∨, →, ↔
Defn

A proposition is a statement that is either true or false (not both).

Propositions: “1 + 1 = 4” (false, still a proposition), “There are infinitely many primes” (true), “Today is Monday.”
Not propositions: “x + 1 = 2” (depends on x; that's a predicate, §3), “Close the door” (a command), “What time is it?” (a question).

We use letters p, q, r, s as propositional variables, and write T for true, F for false.

The six connectives

Connectives build bigger propositions out of smaller ones. Each one is defined by its truth table, so memorize this table:

pq¬pp ∧ qp ∨ qp ⊕ qp → qp ↔ q
TTFTTFTT
TFFFTTFF
FTTFTTTF
FFTFFFTT
Defn Rosen §1.1 Defs 1–4
  • Negation ¬p: “it is not the case that p.” Opposite truth value.
  • Conjunction p ∧ q (“p and q”): true when both are true, false otherwise. In logic, “but” also means ∧.
  • Disjunction p ∨ q (“p or q”): false when both are false, true otherwise. This is the inclusive or.
  • Exclusive or p ⊕ q: true when exactly one of p, q is true.
Caution

In everyday English “or” often means “one or the other but not both.” In math, ∨ always allows both. The lecture joke: a logician's spouse says “you may have cake or you may have pie,” and the logician replies “Excellent! I'll have both.”

Defn Rosen §1.1 Def 5

The conditional (or implication) p → q is the proposition “if p, then q.” It is false only when p is true and q is false, and true otherwise. p is the hypothesis (premise, antecedent); q is the conclusion (consequence).

Picture it

Think of p → q as a promise: “If you get an A on the final, then you get an A in the class.” The only way the promise is broken is if you ace the final and still don't get the A (T → F). If you don't ace the final, the promise hasn't been broken no matter what happens, so the statement counts as true. That's why the bottom two rows are T. We call this vacuously true.

Caution

In English, “if p then q” suggests p causes q. In math there's no causation needed: “If 1 + 1 = 2, then 2 + 2 = 4” is true; “If 1 + 1 = 3, then Santa is real” is also true (false hypothesis).

Principle of explosion

A false proposition implies any proposition. This isn't just a quirk of the truth table: the rules of proof let you derive anything from a false assumption.

Claim: if 1 + 1 = 3 then 1 + 1 = 4. Suppose 1 + 1 = 3. Then 2 = 3, so 2 − 1 = 3 − 1, so 1 = 2. So 1 + 1 = 2 + 2 = 4.

All the ways to say “p → q”

EnglishEnglish
if p, then qp implies q
q if pp is sufficient for q
p only if qq is necessary for p
q whenever pq follows from p
q provided that pq unless ¬p
Caution

“p only if q” means p → q, not q → p. “You can vote only if you're 18” means vote → 18. Being 18 doesn't force you to vote; it's a requirement.

“p unless q” means ¬q → p. Lecture example: “You will not pass the class unless you take the final.” With p = pass, q = take final: ¬q → ¬p, equivalently p → q.

Example translating

“You will see the movie only if you are an adult or have permission.” Let m = see movie, a = adult, p = permission. Answer: m → (a ∨ p).

“We will picnic if it is sunny and we are not tired.” (p = picnic, q = sunny, r = tired) Answer: (q ∧ ¬r) → p. The “if” clause is the hypothesis even though it comes second.

Converse, contrapositive, inverse

Defn

Starting from p → q:

  • converse: q → p
  • contrapositive: ¬q → ¬p  ← always equivalent to p → q
  • inverse: ¬p → ¬q  (equivalent to the converse, not the original)
Example

S a shape. “If S is a square, then S has 4 sides”: true. Converse “If S has 4 sides, then S is a square”: false (rectangles). Contrapositive “If S doesn't have 4 sides, it's not a square”: true, same as the original.

Defn Rosen §1.1 Def 6

The biconditional p ↔ q (“p if and only if q”, abbreviated iff) is true when p and q have the same truth value and false otherwise. It means (p → q) ∧ (q → p).

Order of operations

Precedence, highest to lowest: ¬, ∧, ∨, →, ↔. (Like PEMDAS: ¬ is like a minus sign, ∧ like ×, ∨ like +.)

Example

p ∨ q ∧ r means p ∨ (q ∧ r). With p = T, q = F, r = F this is T, but (p ∨ q) ∧ r is F, so order matters.

¬p ∧ q ∨ p → p ∧ r means ((¬p ∧ q) ∨ p) → (p ∧ r).

Truth tables for compound propositions

With n variables, a truth table has 2n rows. Make a column for each sub-piece, working from the inside out.

Example Lecture 1
pqp ∧ q¬p(p ∧ q) ∨ ¬pp → q
TTTFTT
TFFFFF
FTFTTT
FFFTTT

The last two columns match, so (p ∧ q) ∨ ¬p ≡ p → q. This is the method HW 1.1 asks for: build the table, then read off a simpler formula with the same column.

Warm-up · not graded

  1. Is “x + 2 = 5” a proposition?

    Hint
    Does it have a definite truth value on its own?
    Answer
    No. Its truth depends on x, so it's a predicate (§3).

  2. When exactly is p → q false?

    Answer
    Only when p is true and q is false.

  3. Translate “You get dessert only if you eat your vegetables” (d, v).

    Hint
    “Only if” points at the requirement.
    Answer
    d → v.

Lock it down

How to do it: is it a proposition?
  1. Is it a statement? Questions (“Is it 5?”), commands (“Close the door”) and exclamations are never propositions.
  2. Hunt for loose letters or pronouns. If it has an x, n, “he” or “this number” whose value is never fixed, it is not a proposition yet. It is a predicate.
  3. Ask: could it be marked T or F? If yes, it is a proposition, even if it is false (“7 is even”) or nobody knows the answer yet (“There is life on Mars”).
  4. Check: “false” and “unknown” are both fine. Only “depends on something missing” or “not a claim at all” rules it out.
How to do it: build a truth table
  1. Count the letters. n letters means 2n rows: 2 letters → 4 rows, 3 letters → 8 rows.
  2. Fill the letter columns in the standard order. First letter: top half T, bottom half F. Next letter: halve the blocks again. Last letter: T, F, T, F, …
  3. Add parentheses using precedence ¬, ∧, ∨, →, ↔, so you know exactly which piece is the main connective.
  4. Make one column per piece, inside out. Small pieces first (like ¬p), then pieces built from them, and the whole formula last.
  5. Fill each column by looking only at the two columns it joins. For →, just hunt for rows where the left is T and the right is F. Those rows get F. Every other row gets T.
  6. Check: right number of rows, no two rows with the same letter values.
How to do it: translate English into logic
  1. Name the simple sentences with letters, in the positive form. “I am not tired” becomes ¬r with r = “I am tired.”
  2. Find the glue word and use this list:
    EnglishLogic
    if p, q · q if p · q whenever pp → q
    p only if qp → q
    p is sufficient for qp → q
    p is necessary for qq → p
    p unless q¬q → p
    p but qp ∧ q
    neither p nor q¬p ∧ ¬q
    p if and only if qp ↔ q
  3. Keep groups together. If a piece is “a and b” or “a or b,” wrap it in parentheses before using it in a rule.
  4. Check with the broken promise. An → is false in exactly one situation: left side happens, right side doesn't. Ask: is that the one situation that would make the English sentence a lie? If yes, you translated it right.
How to do it: converse, contrapositive, inverse
  1. Rewrite as “if p, then q” first. Turn “only if,” “unless,” “necessary” into → using the list above. Write down exactly what p and q are.
  2. Converse: swap. q → p.
  3. Inverse: negate both, keep the order. ¬p → ¬q.
  4. Contrapositive: swap and negate. ¬q → ¬p.
  5. Negate compound pieces carefully. ¬(a ∧ b) is ¬a ∨ ¬b, and ¬(a ∨ b) is ¬a ∧ ¬b. The ∧ and ∨ flip.
  6. Check: the contrapositive always has the same truth value as the original. The converse and inverse always match each other.
Example “unless” with a group, then all three relatives

Translate “The app crashes unless you update it and restart it.” Let c = the app crashes, u = you update it, r = you restart it. Then write the converse, inverse and contrapositive.

p = c, q = (u ∧ r)“p unless q”; the group “update and restart” stays together ¬(u ∧ r) → c“p unless q” means ¬q → p converse: c → ¬(u ∧ r)swap the two sides inverse: (u ∧ r) → ¬cnegate both sides, ¬¬ cancels contrapositive: ¬c → (u ∧ r)swap and negate, ¬¬ cancels

Read the contrapositive back: “If the app didn't crash, then you updated it and restarted it.” That says the same thing as the original. The translation could also be written (¬u ∨ ¬r) → c by De Morgan.

Example 3-letter truth table with precedence

Build the truth table for ¬p ∨ q → r. In how many rows is it false?

(¬p ∨ q) → r∨ binds tighter than →, so → is the main connective 8 rows3 letters, 23 = 8 columns: ¬p, then ¬p ∨ q, then the whole thinginside out F exactly where ¬p ∨ q is T and r is Fthe only way an → is false
pqr¬p¬p ∨ q(¬p ∨ q) → r
TTTFTT
TTFFTF
TFTFFT
TFFFFT
FTTTTT
FTFTTF
FFTTTT
FFFTTF

Answer: false in 3 rows (TTF, FTF, FFF). Notice rows 3 and 4: the left side is F, so the → is automatically T no matter what r is.

Common mistakes
  • Wrong: “p only if q” is q → p. Right: p → q. (The part after “only if” is the requirement, so it goes on the right.)
  • Wrong: “p is necessary for q” is p → q. Right: q → p. (If q happens, the necessary thing p must have happened.)
  • Wrong: p → q is false whenever p is false. Right: it is false only when p is T and q is F. (A false hypothesis makes it vacuously true.)
  • Wrong: the contrapositive of p → q is ¬p → ¬q. Right: that is the inverse. The contrapositive is ¬q → ¬p: swap and negate.
  • Wrong: “x > 3” is a proposition because it is math. Right: it is a predicate until x gets a value. (Mathy-looking doesn't mean true-or-false.)

Textbook practice Rosen §1.1: 1, 13, 25, 29, 35 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

§2Equivalences & tautologies

Lecture 1 · Rosen §1.3 · HW 1.4–1.6

Two formulas are equivalent when they always have the same truth value. A short list of equivalences lets you simplify formulas like algebra.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Show two formulas are equivalent, by truth table or by a labeled chain of laws
  • Decide if a formula is a tautology, contradiction or contingency
Memorize
  • p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q ≡ ¬q → ¬p
  • ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q
  • De Morgan's laws
  • Distributive laws
  • p ↔ q ≡ (p → q) ∧ (q → p)
Defn Rosen §1.3 Def 1

A compound proposition that is always true, whatever the truth values of its variables, is a tautology. One that is always false is a contradiction. One that is neither is a contingency.

Examples: p ∨ ¬p is a tautology, p ∧ ¬p is a contradiction, p → q is a contingency.

Defn Rosen §1.3 Def 2

Propositions p and q are logically equivalent, written p ≡ q, if p ↔ q is a tautology. Equivalently: they have identical truth-table columns.

≡ is not a connective. p ≡ q is a statement about two formulas, not a new formula.

The equivalences to know

NameEquivalence
Conditional as “or”p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q
Contrapositivep → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p
Negated conditional¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q
Biconditionalp ↔ q ≡ (p → q) ∧ (q → p)
De Morgan¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∨ ¬q    ¬(p ∨ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q
Double negation¬(¬p) ≡ p
Identityp ∧ T ≡ p    p ∨ F ≡ p
Dominationp ∨ T ≡ T    p ∧ F ≡ F
Negationp ∨ ¬p ≡ T    p ∧ ¬p ≡ F
Idempotentp ∨ p ≡ p    p ∧ p ≡ p
Commutativep ∨ q ≡ q ∨ p    p ∧ q ≡ q ∧ p
Associative(p ∨ q) ∨ r ≡ p ∨ (q ∨ r)    (p ∧ q) ∧ r ≡ p ∧ (q ∧ r)
Distributivep ∧ (q ∨ r) ≡ (p ∧ q) ∨ (p ∧ r)    p ∨ (q ∧ r) ≡ (p ∨ q) ∧ (p ∨ r)
Absorptionp ∨ (p ∧ q) ≡ p    p ∧ (p ∨ q) ≡ p
Same hypothesis(p → q) ∧ (p → r) ≡ p → (q ∧ r)
Same conclusion(p → r) ∧ (q → r) ≡ (p ∨ q) → r
Exportationp → (q → r) ≡ (p ∧ q) → r
Contradiction(¬p → F) ≡ p
Picture it

De Morgan: “Not (rich and famous)” means “not rich, or not famous.” To negate, flip every ∧ ↔ ∨ and push the ¬ inside. The same move works for quantifiers (§3): ∀ ↔ ∃.

¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q: the only way a promise is broken is if p happened and q didn't. This one shows up constantly when setting up proofs by contradiction.

Example Lecture 1: chaining equivalences

Show ¬(p ∨ (¬p ∧ q)) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q without a truth table.

¬(p ∨ (¬p ∧ q)) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬(¬p ∧ q)De Morgan ≡ ¬p ∧ (¬¬p ∨ ¬q)De Morgan ≡ ¬p ∧ (p ∨ ¬q)double negation ≡ (¬p ∧ p) ∨ (¬p ∧ ¬q)distributive ≡ F ∨ (¬p ∧ ¬q)negation law ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬qidentity

Lecture's point: truth tables (like the 8-row one for the distributive law) are “horrible and error-prone”; chaining known equivalences is cleaner. Label each step.

Example two ways to show a tautology

Is p ∨ (q → ¬p) a tautology? Cases: if p is true, the whole “or” is true. If p is false, ¬p is true so q → ¬p is true. Either way: true. Algebra: p ∨ (¬q ∨ ¬p) ≡ (p ∨ ¬p) ∨ ¬q ≡ T ∨ ¬q ≡ T.

Is q ∧ (q → p) a tautology? No: one false row is enough, and q = F makes it false.

Same idea (Lecture 9 review): (p → q) ∨ p is a tautology. If p is true the right side is true; if p is false then p → q is true.

Sidebar P vs NP

Checking a tautology by truth table takes 2n rows. With 400 variables that's more rows than atoms in the universe. Is there a fast (polynomial-time) program to decide whether a formula is a tautology? That's essentially the P = NP question, a $1 million Millennium Prize problem. It matters for computer science because propositional formulas are basically logic circuits.

Warm-up · not graded

  1. Simplify ¬(p ∧ ¬q).

    Hint
    De Morgan, then double negation.
    Answer
    ¬p ∨ q, which is the same as p → q.

  2. Is (p → q) ∨ (q → p) a tautology?

    Hint
    Split on whether q is true.
    Answer
    Yes. If q is true, p → q is true. If q is false, q → p is true.

  3. Write ¬(p → q) without using →.

    Answer
    p ∧ ¬q.

Lock it down

How to do it: equivalence by truth table
  1. List every letter that appears in either formula. n letters means 2n rows.
  2. Build both formulas' columns inside out, in the same table.
  3. Compare the two final columns row by row.
  4. Same in every row: the formulas are equivalent. Write “the columns are identical, so A ≡ B.”
  5. Different in even one row: they are not equivalent. That row is your proof. Write it down, e.g. “p = T, q = F: A is T but B is F.”
How to do it: equivalence by a chain of laws
  1. Start from the messier side. You'll simplify it until it looks like the other side.
  2. Kill → and ↔ first. p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q. p ↔ q ≡ (p → q) ∧ (q → p).
  3. Push every ¬ inward with De Morgan and double negation, until ¬ only sits on single letters.
  4. Tidy up. Use commutative and associative laws to put matching letters next to each other. Use distributive (either direction) to pull out a common letter.
  5. Look for kill pairs. p ∨ ¬p becomes T and p ∧ ¬p becomes F. Then identity and domination wipe them out. Absorption is also a big shortcut.
  6. One law per line, and name it. Graders want the label.
  7. Stuck? Simplify the other side too. If both sides reach the same formula C, you have shown A ≡ C ≡ B.
How to do it: tautology, contradiction or contingency?
  1. Try to make it false. Find one row where it is F? Then it is not a tautology.
  2. Try to make it true. Find one row where it is T? Then it is not a contradiction.
  3. Found both: contingency. Name the two rows as your evidence.
  4. Shortcut when the main connective is →. To make it false you need left = T and right = F. Write down what that forces. If the letters end up needing to be both T and F, no false row exists: it is a tautology.
  5. To prove a tautology, a case split (“if p is T … if p is F …”) or a chain of laws ending in T is enough. Checking only a few rows is not.
Example chain of laws with a common letter

Show (p → q) ∧ (p → ¬q) ≡ ¬p without a truth table.

(p → q) ∧ (p → ¬q)start from the messy side ≡ (¬p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ ¬q)conditional as “or,” used twice ≡ ¬p ∨ (q ∧ ¬q)distributive, read right to left (pull out ¬p) ≡ ¬p ∨ Fnegation law ≡ ¬pidentity law

Sanity check in words: if p were true, you'd need both q and ¬q, which is impossible. So the pair of promises can only hold when p is false.

Example tautology by “try to make it false”

Is [(p → q) ∧ (q → r)] → (p → r) a tautology?

need p → r = Fto make the main → false, the right side must be F so p = T, r = Fthe only way p → r is false need p → q = T, so q = Tthe left side must be T, and p is T need q → r = T, but q = T, r = F gives Fthe left side must be T; it isn't no false row existsevery attempt to make it false breaks

Answer: tautology. (This is exactly why the hypothetical syllogism rule in §5 is allowed.) This took 4 lines instead of an 8-row table.

Common mistakes
  • Wrong: ¬(p ∧ q) ≡ ¬p ∧ ¬q. Right: ¬p ∨ ¬q. (De Morgan flips the connective. “Not both” only means at least one fails.)
  • Wrong: ¬(p → q) ≡ ¬p → ¬q. Right: p ∧ ¬q. (Breaking a promise is an event, an “and,” not another promise.)
  • Wrong: p → q ≡ p ∨ ¬q. Right: ¬p ∨ q. (The ¬ goes on the hypothesis. Test p = F, q = T: p → q is T but p ∨ ¬q is F.)
  • Wrong: p ∧ (q ∨ r) ≡ (p ∧ q) ∨ r. Right: (p ∧ q) ∨ (p ∧ r). (Distributing means p goes to every piece, like 2(a + b) = 2a + 2b.)
  • Wrong: “I checked 3 rows and they were all T, so it's a tautology.” Right: you need every row, or a case split or chain that covers all of them. One F row is enough to say “not a tautology.”

Textbook practice Rosen §1.3: 7, 9, 15, 19, 27 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

§3Predicates & quantifiers

Lecture 2 · Rosen §1.4 · HW 1.7, 1.9

A predicate is a sentence with a blank, like “x > 3.” ∀ means every x works; ∃ means at least one does. To negate, swap ∀ ↔ ∃ and negate what's inside.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Evaluate ∀/∃ statements over a given domain
  • Translate the four patterns (every / some / no / some-not)
  • Push negations inward
Memorize
  • ¬∀x P ≡ ∃x ¬P and ¬∃x P ≡ ∀x ¬P
  • The four patterns: every ∀(P → Q), some ∃(P ∧ Q), no ∀(P → ¬Q), some-not ∃(P ∧ ¬Q)
  • ∀ distributes over ∧; ∃ over ∨ (not the other way)
Defn

A predicate is a statement involving variables which becomes true or false once the values of those variables are specified.

Let P(x) be “x² ≥ x”. Then P(2) is true (4 ≥ 2), P(1) is true (1 ≥ 1), and P(½) is false (¼ ≥ ½ is false). We write predicates with capital letters: P(x), Q(x), L(x, y).

Defn Rosen §1.4 Defs 1–2

∀x P(x) (the universal quantification) is the proposition “P(x) is true for every x in the domain.” An x where P(x) is false is a counterexample.

∃x P(x) (the existential quantification) is the proposition “there exists an x in the domain such that P(x).”

Caution

A quantified statement means nothing until you fix the domain (the allowed values of x). With P(x) = “x² ≥ x”:

  • Domain ℝ: ∀x P(x) is false (x = ½).
  • Domain ℤ: ∀x P(x) is true. If x ≥ 1 then x·x ≥ x·1; if x ≤ 0 then x² ≥ 0 ≥ x.
Picture it

Over a finite domain {a₁, …, aₙ}, quantifiers are just long and/or chains:

∀x P(x) ≡ P(a₁) ∧ P(a₂) ∧ … ∧ P(aₙ)  and  ∃x P(x) ≡ P(a₁) ∨ P(a₂) ∨ … ∨ P(aₙ).

So negating a quantifier is just De Morgan's law on a long chain.

Negating quantifiers

¬∀x P(x) ≡ ∃x ¬P(x)     ¬∃x P(x) ≡ ∀x ¬P(x)

“Not everyone passed” = “someone didn't pass.” “Nobody cheated” = “everyone didn't cheat.”

Example push negations inward

Rewrite ¬∀x [P(x) → Q(x)] so every ¬ sits right in front of a predicate.

¬∀x [P(x) → Q(x)] ≡ ∃x ¬[P(x) → Q(x)]negate ∀ ≡ ∃x ¬[¬P(x) ∨ Q(x)]p → q ≡ ¬p ∨ q ≡ ∃x [¬¬P(x) ∧ ¬Q(x)]De Morgan ≡ ∃x [P(x) ∧ ¬Q(x)]double negation

Read it: “not every P is a Q” = “some P is not a Q.” Shortcut: ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q.

Translating English: the four patterns

The idea

Each of these sentences has two pieces: a group and a claim about that group.

“Every dog is friendly.” Domain: all animals. D(x): x is a dog. F(x): x is friendly.

x runs over every animal, cats and fish included. So the logic has to say “I only care about the dogs.” That's what the → or ∧ is for.

The recipe: 3 questions
  1. Every or some? “every / all / no” → ∀x. “some / there is / at least one” → ∃x.
  2. Which glue? ∀ takes → (“if it's in the group, then …”). ∃ takes ∧ (“it's in the group and …”).
  3. Is there a “no” or “not”? Put ¬ on the claim.

The group always goes first: D(x) on the left, the claim on the right.

Every dog is friendly

∀x (D(x) → F(x))

Check every animal: if it's a dog, then it's friendly.

Cats don't matter. For a cat “if dog” is false, so the → is automatically true.

Some dog is friendly

∃x (D(x) ∧ F(x))

There's an animal that is a dog and is friendly.

You need one animal that ticks both boxes.

No dog is friendly

∀x (D(x) → ¬F(x))

Check every animal: if it's a dog, then it's not friendly.

Same thing: ¬∃x (D(x) ∧ F(x)), “there is no friendly dog.”

Some dog is not friendly

∃x (D(x) ∧ ¬F(x))

There's an animal that is a dog and is not friendly.

One grumpy dog is enough.

Example using the recipe

Translate “No student cheated.” Domain: all people. S(x): x is a student. C(x): x cheated.

group = students, claim = cheatedfind the two pieces ∀x“no” is an every-type word ∀x (S(x) → C(x))∀ takes →, group on the left ∀x (S(x) → ¬C(x))“no” puts ¬ on the claim

Read it back: “Check every person: if they're a student, they didn't cheat.” ✓

Why not swap the glue?

∀ is a security guard checking every animal in the building. The rule is only for dogs, so the guard asks “if you're a dog, are you friendly?” and waves the cats through. That's →.

∃ is a scavenger hunt: find one animal that is a dog and friendly. Both boxes on the same animal. That's ∧.

Swap them and it breaks:

  • ∀x (D(x) ∧ F(x)) says “every animal is a friendly dog.” One cat makes it false. Too strong.
  • ∃x (D(x) → F(x)) is true as soon as one cat exists, because for a cat “if dog …” is false, which makes the → true. Too weak: it says nothing about dogs.
They come in opposite pairs
Every dog is friendlyoppositeSome dog is not friendly Some dog is friendlyoppositeNo dog is friendly

So “not every dog is friendly” means “some dog is not friendly,” and “it's not true that some dog is friendly” means “no dog is friendly.” This is the ¬∀ ≡ ∃¬ rule from above: ¬∀x (D(x) → F(x)) ≡ ∃x (D(x) ∧ ¬F(x)).

PatternAlso written asLogic
Everyall, each, any, “dogs are friendly”∀x (D → F)
Somethere is, at least one, a few∃x (D ∧ F)
Nonone, not a single, there is no∀x (D → ¬F)
Some-notnot every, not all, at least one … isn't∃x (D ∧ ¬F)
Trap: only“Only dogs bark” = if it barks, it's a dog∀x (B → D)

When do quantifiers distribute?

Works

∀x (P(x) ∧ Q(x)) ≡ ∀x P(x) ∧ ∀x Q(x)

∃x (P(x) ∨ Q(x)) ≡ ∃x P(x) ∨ ∃x Q(x)

Fails

∀x (P ∨ Q) ≢ ∀x P ∨ ∀x Q. Over ℤ: ∀x (x ≥ 0 ∨ x ≤ 0) is true but ∀x (x ≥ 0) ∨ ∀x (x ≤ 0) is false.

∃x (P ∧ Q) ≢ ∃x P ∧ ∃x Q. ∃x (x = 1 ∧ x = 2) is false but ∃x (x = 1) ∧ ∃x (x = 2) is true.

Memory trick: ∀ is a big ∧, so it plays nicely with ∧. ∃ is a big ∨, so it plays nicely with ∨.

English words for ∀ and ∃

∃x P(x)∀x P(x)
there exists x such that P(x)for all x, P(x)
some x satisfies P(x)every / each x satisfies P(x)
at least one x satisfies P(x)given an arbitrary x, P(x) is true
we can find x with P(x)suppose x is in the domain; then P(x)
Caution

Avoid “any” and “some” in your own writing. “Any” can mean ∀ (“any integer squared is ≥ 0”) or ∃ (“is there any solution?”). The lecture's advice: just say “there exists” and “for all.”

Quantifying over a set

Defn

(∀x ∈ A) P(x) abbreviates ∀x (x ∈ A → P(x)).   (∃x ∈ A) P(x) abbreviates ∃x (x ∈ A ∧ P(x)).

Examples: (∀x ∈ ℝ)(x > 0) false; (∀x ∈ ℝ)(x² ≥ 0) true; (∀x ∈ ℕ)(x > 0) false (x = 0); (∃x ∈ ℕ)(x − 1 = 0) true.

Over the empty set: (∀x ∈ ∅) P(x) is always true (vacuously: there is no x to break it), and (∃x ∈ ∅) P(x) is always false (there's nothing to be the witness).

Warm-up · not graded

  1. Domain ℤ: true or false, ∃x (x² = 2)?

    Answer
    False. √2 isn't an integer.

  2. Negate “Every student passed.”

    Answer
    “Some student didn't pass”: ∃x ¬P(x).

  3. Translate “Some dog is not friendly” using D(x), F(x).

    Hint
    ∃ goes with ∧.
    Answer
    ∃x (D(x) ∧ ¬F(x)).

Textbook practice Rosen §1.4: 9, 13, 23, 35, 37 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

§4Nested quantifiers

Lecture 2 · Rosen §1.5 · HW 1.8–1.9

With two quantifiers, order matters. ∀x ∃y lets y depend on x; ∃y ∀x needs a single y that works for every x.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Read and translate nested quantifiers
  • Tell ∀x∃y from ∃y∀x and give an example where they differ
  • Negate nested statements
  • Write “exactly one” in symbols
Memorize
  • Swapping ∀ and ∃ changes the meaning
  • ∃y∀x P ⇒ ∀x∃y P (not the converse)
  • ∃!x P(x) ≡ ∃x[P(x) ∧ ∀y(y ≠ x → ¬P(y))]
Example L(x, y): “x loves y”
  • ∀x ∃y L(x, y): everyone loves someone.
  • ∃x ∀y L(x, y): someone loves everyone.
  • ∃x ∃y L(x, y): someone loves someone.
  • ∃y ∀x L(x, y): there's one person loved by everyone (the lecture's answer: Dolly Parton).
Quantifier order

Swapping two quantifiers of the same kind is fine: ∀x ∀y P ≡ ∀y ∀x P and ∃x ∃y P ≡ ∃y ∃x P.

Swapping a ∀ and an ∃ changes the meaning: ∀x ∃y P(x, y) ≢ ∃y ∀x P(x, y).

One direction does hold: ∃y ∀x P(x, y) implies ∀x ∃y P(x, y), but not the reverse.

Picture it

∀x ∃y: “every lock has a key.” Each lock may need a different key, and y is allowed to depend on x.
∃y ∀x: “there's a master key.” One fixed y works for every x at once.

A master key gives every lock a key, but every lock having a key doesn't give you a master key. Another way to see it: read the quantifiers left to right as a game. For ∀x ∃y, an opponent picks x first and then you get to respond with y.

Example integers

∀x ∃y (y > x): “every integer has a bigger integer.” True (take y = x + 1).

∃y ∀x (y > x): “some integer is bigger than all integers.” False (it would have to be bigger than itself).

Domain {1, 2, 3} instead: ∃y ∀x (y ≥ x) is true, with y = 3.

Lecture 9 review: a predicate on ℝ where ∀x ∃y P(x, y) is true but ∃y ∀x P(x, y) is false is P(x, y): x < y.

Example negating nested quantifiers

Flip each quantifier as the ¬ passes through it, then negate what's inside:

¬∀x ∃y P(x, y) ≡ ∃x ∀y ¬P(x, y)

¬∀x ∀y (P(x, y) → Q(x, y)) ≡ ∃x ∃y (P(x, y) ∧ ¬Q(x, y))

“There is a unique x”

Defn

∃!x P(x) (the uniqueness quantifier) means “there is exactly one x with P(x).” In ordinary quantifiers:

∃!x P(x) ≡ ∃x [ P(x) ∧ ∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y)) ]

“There is an x with P(x), and every other y fails P.” (Equivalently ∃x [P(x) ∧ ∀y (P(y) → y = x)].)

Example Lecture 2 exercise
¬∃x [P(x) ∧ ∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y))] ≡ ∀x ¬[P(x) ∧ ∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y))]negate ∃ ≡ ∀x [¬P(x) ∨ ¬∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y))]De Morgan ≡ ∀x [¬P(x) ∨ ∃y ¬(y ≠ x → ¬P(y))]negate ∀ ≡ ∀x [¬P(x) ∨ ∃y (y ≠ x ∧ P(y))]¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q

Meaning: every x either fails P or has a different y that also satisfies P. So there are zero solutions, or at least two.

Warm-up · not graded

  1. Domain ℝ. Is ∀x ∃y (x + y = 0) true? Is ∃y ∀x (x + y = 0)?

    Hint
    In the second, one fixed y has to work for every x.
    Answer
    First: true (take y = −x). Second: false (no single y cancels every x).

  2. Negate ∀x ∃y (y > x²).

    Hint
    Flip each quantifier, then negate the inside.
    Answer
    ∃x ∀y (y ≤ x²).

Lock it down

How to do it: read and translate nested quantifiers
  1. Symbols to English: go left to right. Say “for every x” for ∀x and “there is a y” for ∃y, then say the predicate. ∀x ∃y L(x, y): “for every x there is a y such that x loves y.”
  2. Then smooth it out. “Everyone loves someone.” Keep track of which slot is which: in L(x, y) the first slot does the loving.
  3. English to symbols: find the two roles. Who is “every” and who is “some”? Which slot of the predicate does each fill?
  4. Decide the order. If the “some” thing is allowed to be different for each “every” thing, the ∃ comes after the ∀. If it must be one single thing for all, the ∃ comes first.
  5. Negative words: “nobody” and “no one” become ∀ … ¬, as in §3.
  6. Check: read your symbols back left to right and see if you get the sentence.
How to do it: decide true or false (the game)
  1. Two players. ∀ is your opponent's move (they pick, trying to break it). ∃ is your move (you pick, trying to make it work).
  2. Moves happen left to right. You may use any value already picked. In ∀x ∃y, you see x before choosing y, so y can be a formula in x.
  3. Prove ∀x ∃y true: give a rule for y in terms of x that always works, like y = x + 1.
  4. Prove ∃y ∀x true: name one fixed y and show it works for every x.
  5. Prove something false: show its negation is true. Negate it (next recipe), then play the game for the negation.
  6. Need an example where the orders differ? Use one where the answer y must change with x: y > x on ℤ, or x + y = 0 on ℝ.
How to do it: negate a nested statement
  1. Slide the ¬ to the right one quantifier at a time. Each time it passes a quantifier, flip it: ∀ becomes ∃ and ∃ becomes ∀.
  2. Keep the order and the variables exactly the same. Only the symbols ∀ and ∃ change.
  3. When the ¬ reaches the inside, negate it with §2 rules: ¬(A → B) ≡ A ∧ ¬B, ¬(A ∧ B) ≡ ¬A ∨ ¬B, ¬(A ∨ B) ≡ ¬A ∧ ¬B, ¬(x < y) ≡ x ≥ y.
  4. Done when no ¬ sits in front of a quantifier or a bracket.
  5. Check: same number of quantifiers, same order, every one flipped.
How to do it: write “exactly one,” “at most one,” “at least two”
  1. Split “exactly one” into two parts: at least one, and no others.
  2. At least one: ∃x P(x).
  3. No others: ∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y)). This must sit inside the brackets of ∃x, because it talks about that same x.
  4. Glue with ∧: ∃x [P(x) ∧ ∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y))].
  5. Related shapes: at most one is ∀x ∀y ((P(x) ∧ P(y)) → x = y). At least two is ∃x ∃y (x ≠ y ∧ P(x) ∧ P(y)).
  6. Check: picture a world with 0, 1 and 2 objects that have P. “Exactly one” should be true only in the world with 1.
Example playing the game on ℤ

Domain ℤ. Decide (a) ∀x ∃y (x = 2y) and (b) ∃x ∀y (xy = x).

(a) opponent picks x first∀x is their move opponent picks x = 1they try to break it need 2y = 1, so y = 1/2, not an integerno legal move for us (a) is falseits negation ∃x ∀y (x ≠ 2y) holds with x = 1 (b) we pick x first, then any y comes∃x is our move, ∀y theirs pick x = 0: then xy = 0 = x for every yone fixed x that beats every y (b) is truewe named a winning x

Compare: ∀x ∃y (y = 2x) is true (take y = 2x). Swapping which variable is “the double” changed the answer, so read the predicate carefully.

Example exactly one, nested, then negated

x ranges over students, y and z over professors. A(x, y): y is an advisor of x. Write “Every student has exactly one advisor,” then negate it and say the negation in English.

∀x [ … ]“every student” is the outside quantifier ∃y [A(x, y) ∧ ∀z (z ≠ y → ¬A(x, z))]“exactly one advisor” for that x, with the recipe ∀x ∃y [A(x, y) ∧ ∀z (z ≠ y → ¬A(x, z))]put it together ¬ … ≡ ∃x ∀y ¬[A(x, y) ∧ ∀z (z ≠ y → ¬A(x, z))]flip ∀x and ∃y ≡ ∃x ∀y [¬A(x, y) ∨ ¬∀z (z ≠ y → ¬A(x, z))]De Morgan ≡ ∃x ∀y [¬A(x, y) ∨ ∃z (z ≠ y ∧ A(x, z))]flip ∀z, then ¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q

Read back: “There is a student x such that every professor y either isn't x's advisor, or x has a second advisor z.” In plain English: some student has no advisor or has at least two.

Common mistakes
  • Wrong: negating only the first quantifier, ¬∀x ∃y P ≡ ∃x ∃y ¬P. Right: ∃x ∀y ¬P. (The ¬ walks past every quantifier and flips each one.)
  • Wrong: reordering while negating, ¬∀x ∃y P ≡ ∃y ∀x ¬P. Right: keep x first: ∃x ∀y ¬P. (Order carries meaning; the negation never moves letters.)
  • Wrong: to show ∃x ∀y P(x, y) is true, “pick y = 0.” Right: y is the opponent's choice. You only pick x, and it has to work for every y.
  • Wrong: “exactly one” as ∃x ∀y (P(y) → y = x). Right: add P(x) ∧ inside. (Without it, the formula is also true when nothing has P, so it only says “at most one.”)
  • Wrong: ∃x P(x) ∧ ∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y)) with the bracket closed early. Right: ∃x [P(x) ∧ ∀y (y ≠ x → ¬P(y))]. (Outside the bracket, the second x is a stranger, not the one you found.)

Textbook practice Rosen §1.5: 9, 25, 27, 31, 39 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

§5Rules of inference

Lecture 2 · Rosen §1.6 · HW 1.10–1.11

Rules of inference are the legal moves for getting new facts from ones you have. A proof is a chain of legal moves.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Write a numbered argument from premises
  • Spot the two fallacies
Memorize
  • Modus ponens and tollens, hypothetical and disjunctive syllogism (names not tested)
  • The four quantifier rules (instantiation and generalization)
Defn

A (propositional) rule of inference is a tautology of the form (q₁ ∧ … ∧ qₙ) → p, expressing that if q₁, …, qₙ are all true then p is true. It's often written with premises stacked above a line and the conclusion after “∴”.

A propositional argument is a list of propositions where each line is a premise or follows from earlier lines by a rule of inference.

Rosen §1.6 Def 1: an argument is a sequence of propositions; all but the last are premises, the last is the conclusion. It is valid if the truth of all the premises implies the conclusion is true.

RuleFromConcludeTautology
Modus ponensp,  p → qq(p ∧ (p → q)) → q
Modus tollens¬q,  p → q¬p(¬q ∧ (p → q)) → ¬p
Hypothetical syllogismp → q,  q → rp → r((p → q) ∧ (q → r)) → (p → r)
Disjunctive syllogismp ∨ q,  ¬pq((p ∨ q) ∧ ¬p) → q
Simplificationp ∧ qp(p ∧ q) → p
Additionpp ∨ qp → (p ∨ q)
Conjunctionp,  qp ∧ q((p) ∧ (q)) → (p ∧ q)
Resolutionp ∨ q,  ¬p ∨ rq ∨ r((p ∨ q) ∧ (¬p ∨ r)) → (q ∨ r)

Lecture: you don't need these names for exams or quizzes. (HW 1.10 still asks for them.)

Picture it

Modus ponens (“affirming”): if it rains the ground is wet; it rains; so the ground is wet.
Modus tollens (“denying”): if it rains the ground is wet; the ground is not wet; so it didn't rain. (This is the contrapositive in action.)
Disjunctive syllogism: the cookie is in the jar or on the plate; it's not in the jar; so it's on the plate.

Caution fallacies (Rosen §1.6)

Affirming the conclusion: from p → q and q, concluding p. “If it rained, the grass is wet. The grass is wet. So it rained.” Wrong: maybe the sprinklers ran. ((p → q) ∧ q) → p is not a tautology.

Denying the hypothesis: from p → q and ¬p, concluding ¬q. “It didn't rain, so the grass isn't wet.” Also invalid.

Both mistakes amount to treating p → q as if it were its converse.

Example Lecture 2

Premises: ¬p ∧ q,  r → p,  ¬r → s,  s → t. Show t.

#StatementReason
1¬p ∧ qpremise
2¬psimplification (1)
3r → ppremise
4¬rmodus tollens (2, 3)
5¬r → spremise
6smodus ponens (4, 5)
7s → tpremise
8tmodus ponens (6, 7)

Strategy for English versions (like HW 1.11): name each simple sentence with a letter, translate every hypothesis, then chain rules.

Rules for quantifiers

RuleFromConclude
Universal instantiation∀x P(x)P(c) for any c in the domain
Universal generalizationP(c) for an arbitrary c∀x P(x)
Existential instantiation∃x P(x)P(c) for some c (a new name, nothing else assumed about it)
Existential generalizationP(c) for some particular c∃x P(x)
Caution

Universal generalization needs c to be truly arbitrary: you may not assume anything special about it. Checking P(2), P(3) and P(10) proves nothing about ∀x P(x). This is why proofs start with “Let n be an arbitrary integer.”

Example Lecture 2

If ∀x (P(x) ∧ Q(x)) then ∀x P(x) ∧ ∀x Q(x).

  1. ∀x (P(x) ∧ Q(x)) — premise
  2. P(c) ∧ Q(c), c arbitrary — universal instantiation
  3. P(c) — simplification
  4. ∀x P(x) — universal generalization
  5. Q(c) — simplification (2)
  6. ∀x Q(x) — universal generalization
  7. ∀x P(x) ∧ ∀x Q(x) — conjunction
Sidebar “the rules of math”

All of mathematics = valid propositional rules of inference + the four quantifier rules + an axiom system (usually ZFC set theory). Russell and Whitehead's Principia Mathematica (1910) tried to do math fully formally this way, and needed hundreds of pages to reach 1 + 1 = 2.

Warm-up · not graded

  1. “If it's a weekday, I have class. I don't have class.” What follows?

    Answer
    It isn't a weekday (modus tollens).

  2. Valid? “If I study, I pass. I passed. So I studied.”

    Answer
    Invalid: that's affirming the conclusion. You could have passed without studying.

Lock it down

How to do it: write a numbered argument
  1. If it's in English, translate first. Give each simple sentence a letter, write every premise in symbols, and write the goal.
  2. Work backwards on scrap paper. Find a premise that contains the goal. If the goal is t and you have s → t, your new mini-goal is s. Repeat until you reach something you already have.
  3. Now write it forwards. One statement per line, numbered. Every line is either “premise” or “rule name (line numbers it uses).”
  4. Use the right tool for each shape. Have a ∧ b? Simplification splits it. Have a → b? Modus ponens if you have a, modus tollens if you have ¬b. Have a ∨ b? Disjunctive syllogism once you have ¬a.
  5. Reshape when needed. You may replace a line by an equivalent one (De Morgan, contrapositive, double negation). Cite the law.
  6. Check: each line uses only earlier lines, and the last line is exactly the goal.
How to do it: valid or fallacy?
  1. Translate to symbols. Rewrite “only if,” “unless,” “necessary” as → first (see §1). Find the conditional p → q and the other premise.
  2. Match the pattern:
    You haveYou concludeVerdict
    pqvalid (modus ponens)
    ¬q¬pvalid (modus tollens)
    qpfallacy: affirming the conclusion
    ¬p¬qfallacy: denying the hypothesis
  3. To prove it's invalid, give one row where every premise is T and the conclusion is F. For both fallacies, the row p = F, q = T works.
  4. Remember: valid is about the form, not about the facts. A valid argument with a false premise can have a false conclusion.
How to do it: arguments with quantifiers
  1. Strip the ∃ premises first. From ∃x P(x), write P(a) with a brand-new name a (existential instantiation). A second ∃ premise needs a second new name.
  2. Then use the ∀ premises on that name. From ∀x (P(x) → Q(x)), write P(a) → Q(a) (universal instantiation). ∀ works for any name, even one you just made up.
  3. Do the propositional moves (modus ponens, simplification, …) on the instantiated lines.
  4. Put a quantifier back at the end. If a was a specific object, use existential generalization to get ∃x. Only if a was arbitrary (you assumed nothing about it) may you use universal generalization to get ∀x.
  5. Shortcut: ∀x (P(x) → Q(x)) plus P(a) gives Q(a) in one go (universal modus ponens).
Example English argument with De Morgan

“If I take the bus or the train, I'll be late. I'm not late. If I don't take the bus, I walk.” Show “I walk.” Letters: b = I take the bus, t = I take the train, l = I'm late, w = I walk. Premises: (b ∨ t) → l,  ¬l,  ¬b → w.

1. (b ∨ t) → lpremise 2. ¬lpremise 3. ¬(b ∨ t)modus tollens (1, 2) 4. ¬b ∧ ¬tDe Morgan (3) 5. ¬bsimplification (4) 6. ¬b → wpremise 7. wmodus ponens (5, 6)

Backwards plan that found this: the goal w only appears in ¬b → w, so I needed ¬b. The only way to get ¬b was to break open ¬(b ∨ t), which came from ¬l.

Example quantifier rules in a row

“Every Math 55 student has taken calculus. Someone in this room is a Math 55 student. So someone in this room has taken calculus.” Domain: people. M(x): x is a Math 55 student. C(x): x has taken calculus. R(x): x is in this room.

1. ∃x (R(x) ∧ M(x))premise 2. R(a) ∧ M(a)existential instantiation (1), a is a new name 3. ∀x (M(x) → C(x))premise 4. M(a) → C(a)universal instantiation (3) 5. M(a)simplification (2) 6. C(a)modus ponens (4, 5) 7. R(a)simplification (2) 8. R(a) ∧ C(a)conjunction (6, 7) 9. ∃x (R(x) ∧ C(x))existential generalization (8)

Notice the order: the ∃ premise was used first so that a was new. And the last step is ∃, not ∀, because a is one particular person.

Common mistakes
  • Wrong: from (p ∧ q) → r and p, conclude r. Right: you need p ∧ q first. (Get q, then use conjunction, then modus ponens.)
  • Wrong: from ¬(p ∧ q), conclude ¬p. Right: De Morgan gives only ¬p ∨ ¬q. (“Not both” doesn't say which one failed.)
  • Wrong: from p ∨ q and p, conclude ¬q. Right: nothing follows about q. (∨ is inclusive: both can be true.)
  • Wrong: from ∃x P(x) and ∃x Q(x), write P(c) and Q(c) with the same c. Right: use a new name for each: P(c), Q(d). (The thing with P and the thing with Q may be different.)
  • Wrong: “the argument is valid, so the conclusion is true.” Right: valid only means if all premises are true, the conclusion is true. A false premise can lead validly to a false conclusion.

Textbook practice Rosen §1.6: 3, 5, 9, 15, 23 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

Part II

Proofs

§6Vocabulary & direct proof

Lecture 3 · Rosen §1.7 · HW 2.1

A proof is an argument built from definitions. A direct proof assumes the “if” part and works forward to the “then” part.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Write a direct proof straight from the definitions
  • Use fresh variables for each number
Memorize
  • even: n = 2k; odd: n = 2k + 1
  • rational: p/q with q ≠ 0; irrational: real and not rational
WordMeaning
TheoremA proposition we can prove is true; usually signals importance. (“Thm”)
LemmaA proven helper result used to prove a bigger theorem.
CorollarySomething that follows easily from a theorem just proved. (“Cor”)
PropositionAlso (confusingly) used for a less important theorem. the professor won't use it this way.
ConjectureBelieved true but not yet proved (e.g. the Twin Prime Conjecture).
ProofThe argument for why it's true; often ends with □ (the “Halmos” tombstone).
AxiomA statement assumed true without proof (Rosen).

The most common theorem shape is ∀x (P(x) → Q(x)): “every object of type P has property Q.” The Pythagorean theorem (every right triangle satisfies a² + b² = c²), Fermat's Last Theorem, and the Four Color Theorem all have this form.

Defn Rosen §1.7 Def 1

An integer n is even if there exists an integer k such that n = 2k, and odd if there exists an integer k such that n = 2k + 1.

Two integers have the same parity if both are even or both are odd, and opposite parity otherwise. Every integer is even or odd, and none is both. (Lecture: we'll prove that later by induction.)

Defn Rosen §1.7 Def 2

A real number r is rational if there exist integers p and q with q ≠ 0 such that r = p/q. A real number that is not rational is irrational.

Defn Direct proof

A direct proof of p → q: assume p is true, then use definitions, known facts and logic to show q must be true.

To prove ∀x (P(x) → Q(x)) directly: let x be an arbitrary element of the domain satisfying P(x), then prove Q(x).

Picture it the recipe
  1. Write down what you're assuming, unpacked with the definition: “n is odd, so n = 2k + 1 for some integer k.”
  2. Write down what you want, also unpacked: “I need n² = 2(something) + 1.”
  3. Do algebra until the thing you have looks like the thing you want.

Most Math 55 proofs at this stage are just “plug in the definition and simplify.”

Example Lecture 3

Thm. For all integers n, if n is even then n² is even.

Assume n is even, so n = 2k for some integer k. Then n² = (2k)² = 4k² = 2(2k²). Since 2k² is an integer, n² is even.

Thm. If n is odd, then n² is odd.

Assume n is odd, so n = 2k + 1 for some integer k. Then n² = 4k² + 4k + 1 = 2(2k² + 2k) + 1, so n² is odd.

Thm. If a, b, c are odd integers, then a + b + c is odd.

Assume a, b, c are odd. By definition a = 2k + 1, b = 2m + 1, c = 2n + 1 for some integers k, m, n. So a + b + c = 2(k + m + n) + 3 = 2(k + m + n + 1) + 1, which is odd.

Caution

Notice the last proof uses three different letters k, m, n. If you wrote a = 2k + 1 and b = 2k + 1 with the same k, you'd be secretly assuming a = b. Each number gets its own fresh variable.

Example Lecture 3

Thm. If r and s are rational, then r + s is rational.

Assume r, s are rational. By definition r = p/q and s = n/m for integers p, q, m, n with q ≠ 0 and m ≠ 0. Then r + s = mp/(mq) + nq/(mq) = (mp + nq)/(mq). Since mp + nq and mq are integers and mq ≠ 0, r + s is rational by definition.

Two degenerate kinds of proof

From Rosen §1.7. A vacuous proof shows p → q by showing p is false (e.g. “if n > 1 then n² > n” is vacuously true for n = 0). A trivial proof shows p → q by showing q is true, without using p.

Proving “if and only if”

To prove p ↔ q, prove both p → q and q → p, usually as two labeled parts “(⇒)” and “(⇐)”. Each direction can use a different technique.

Warm-up · not graded

  1. Prove the sum of two even integers is even.

    Hint
    Call them 2a and 2b, using two different letters.
    Answer
    2a + 2b = 2(a + b), and a + b is an integer.

  2. Prove: if n is odd, then 3n + 1 is even.

    Answer
    n = 2k + 1, so 3n + 1 = 6k + 4 = 2(3k + 2).

Lock it down

How to do it: write a direct proof from the definitions
  1. Find p and q. Rewrite the claim as “for every n, if p then q.” Example: “the product of two odd numbers is odd” means “if m and n are odd, then mn is odd.”
  2. First line: “Assume p.” Name the objects: “Let m and n be odd integers.” Do not pick a specific number like 3. They must be arbitrary.
  3. Unpack p with the definition. Odd means = 2(integer) + 1. Even means = 2(integer). Rational means = p/q with integers p, q and q ≠ 0. Write “for some integer k.”
  4. Write the target on scratch paper. What does q look like unpacked? “I need mn = 2(something) + 1.”
  5. Do algebra. Substitute, expand, then factor out a 2 (or combine into one fraction) so it matches the target shape.
  6. Say why the “something” qualifies. “Since 2ab + a + b is an integer, …” or “since the bottom is a nonzero integer, …”.
  7. Last line: “so mn is odd.” That is q, word for word.
  8. Check: plug in a small number (like k = 1) to test your algebra. This is a check only, never the proof.
How to do it: use fresh variables
  1. List every number in the hypothesis: a, b, c, ….
  2. Give each one its own letter when you unpack it: a = 2k + 1, b = 2j + 1. Never reuse k.
  3. Do not reuse a letter that already means something. If n is in the theorem, do not write “m = 2n.” Pick a new letter.
  4. Say what each letter is: “for some integers k and j.”
  5. Check: could your setup describe two different numbers, like a = 3 and b = 7? If it forces a = b, you reused a letter.
How to do it: prove a number is rational
  1. Unpack each rational with its own letters: x = a/b, y = c/d, where a, b, c, d are integers, b ≠ 0, d ≠ 0.
  2. Combine into one fraction: (top)/(bottom).
  3. Say the top and bottom are integers (sums and products of integers are integers).
  4. Say the bottom is not 0. A product of nonzero numbers is nonzero.
  5. Conclude: “so it is rational by definition.” You do not need lowest terms.
Example two different odd numbers

Thm. If m and n are odd integers, then mn is odd.

Assume m and n are odd integers.first line: assume p Then m = 2a + 1 and n = 2b + 1 for some integers a, b.definition of odd, two fresh letters mn = (2a + 1)(2b + 1) = 4ab + 2a + 2b + 1expand (FOIL) = 2(2ab + a + b) + 1factor a 2 out of everything but the 1 2ab + a + b is an integer.built from integers by + and × So mn is odd. □matches 2(integer) + 1

Check with a = 1, b = 2: m = 3, n = 5, mn = 15 = 2(7) + 1, and 2ab + a + b = 4 + 1 + 2 = 7. ✓

Example a quotient of rationals

Thm. If x and y are rational and y ≠ 0, then x/y is rational.

Assume x, y are rational and y ≠ 0.assume p (all of it) x = a/b and y = c/d for integers a, b, c, d with b ≠ 0, d ≠ 0.definition of rational, fresh letters c ≠ 0if c = 0 then y = 0, but y ≠ 0 x/y = (a/b) · (d/c) = ad/(bc)dividing = multiplying by the flip ad and bc are integers.products of integers bc ≠ 0b ≠ 0 and c ≠ 0 So x/y is rational. □integer over nonzero integer

The step “c ≠ 0” is where the hypothesis y ≠ 0 gets used. If a proof never uses a hypothesis, ask why it was there.

Common mistakes
  • Wrong: “Let n = 3. Then n² = 9 is odd. □” Right: “Let n be odd, so n = 2k + 1 for some integer k.” (Examples check a few cases. A “for all” proof must cover every n.)
  • Wrong: starting from the conclusion: “mn is odd, so mn = 2k + 1, so …”. Right: start from what you are given and work toward the conclusion. (Starting from q proves q → p, the converse.)
  • Wrong: “n = 2k + 1” with no comment. Right: “n = 2k + 1 for some integer k.” (If k could be 1/2, then 2k + 1 = 2 would count as odd.)
  • Wrong: stopping at “n² = 2(2k² + 2k) + 1.” Right: add “since 2k² + 2k is an integer, n² is odd.” (The proof is not done until you say the definition is met.)
  • Wrong: “irrational means it has infinitely many decimals.” Right: irrational means real and not of the form p/q. (1/3 = 0.333… has infinitely many decimals and is rational.)

Textbook practice Rosen §1.7: 1, 3, 5, 7 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

§7Contraposition & contradiction

Lecture 3 · Rosen §1.7 · HW 2.2–2.5

Two indirect strategies: prove the contrapositive (¬q → ¬p), or assume the statement is false and reach something impossible.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Prove by contraposition and by contradiction, saying so in the first line
  • Prove √2 is irrational from memory
Memorize
  • p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p
  • To contradict p → q, assume p ∧ ¬q
  • Required opening phrases for each method
Defn Proof by contraposition

Since p → q ≡ ¬q → ¬p, to prove p implies q we can instead prove ¬q implies ¬p. This is proof by contraposition.

Start the proof with “By contraposition.” and state the contrapositive you're proving.

When to use it

Use contraposition when the hypothesis is hard to unpack but the negated conclusion is easy. “n² is even” gives you nothing about n directly. “n is not even,” i.e. “n is odd,” gives you n = 2k + 1, which you can work with.

Example Lecture 3

Thm. If n² is even, then n is even.

By contraposition. We show: if n is not even, then n² is not even. Assume n is not even, so n is odd. By the earlier theorem, n² is odd, so n² is not even.

Thm. If a, b are reals and ab ≠ 0, then a ≠ 0 and b ≠ 0.

By contraposition. We need: if it's not the case that (a ≠ 0 and b ≠ 0), then ab = 0. By De Morgan, that hypothesis is “a = 0 or b = 0.” If a = 0 then ab = 0, and if b = 0 then ab = 0. So a = 0 or b = 0 implies ab = 0.

Thm (Lecture 9 review). If n, m are integers and n² + 2m is odd, then n is odd.

By contraposition. We show: if n is even then n² + 2m is even. If n = 2k, then n² + 2m = 4k² + 2m = 2(2k² + m), which is even.

Defn Proof by contradiction

To prove p, assume ¬p and derive something false (a contradiction). This works because (¬p → F) ≡ p.

Begin with “Assume for a contradiction that …” and end with “Contradiction!” plus a sentence saying what the contradiction is.

Caution setting up a contradiction for p → q

The negation of p → q is p ∧ ¬q, not “¬p → ¬q.” So you assume p is true and q is false.

Lecture 9: to prove “if x + y is irrational, then x is irrational or y is irrational” by contradiction, assume x + y is irrational and x is rational and y is rational. (De Morgan turned “not (x irrational or y irrational)” into “x rational and y rational.”)

Example Lecture 3

Thm. If a, b, c are integers, then at least one of a + b, a + c, b + c is even.

Assume for a contradiction that a, b, c are integers and a + b, a + c, b + c are all odd. A sum of three odd integers is odd, so (a + b) + (a + c) + (b + c) is odd. But it equals 2a + 2b + 2c = 2(a + b + c), which is even. Contradiction! It can't be both even and odd.

Thm √2 is irrational (know this proof)

Suppose for a contradiction that √2 is rational. Then we can write √2 = p/q as a fraction in lowest terms, so p and q share no common factors; in particular p and q are not both even. Squaring, 2 = p²/q², so p² = 2q², so p² is even. Since p² is even, p is even (theorem above), so p = 2k for some k. Then (2k)² = 2q², so 4k² = 2q², so q² = 2k², so q² is even, so q is even. Contradiction! p and q are both even.

Sidebar

Legend has it that Hippasus, a Pythagorean, first proved √2 is irrational. The others believed every number was rational, so they took him “fishing” and threw him overboard.

Fermat's Last Theorem (claimed 1637, proved by Andrew Wiles 1995): for an integer n > 2, xⁿ + yⁿ = zⁿ has no solutions in positive integers. Lecture shows ∛2 irrational as a corollary: if ∛2 = p/q then 2q³ = p³, i.e. q³ + q³ = p³, violating FLT. (HW 2.5 asks for a proof that avoids FLT.)

ContrapositionContradiction
Provesan implication p → qany statement p
You assume¬q¬p  (for p → q: assume p ∧ ¬q)
You must reachspecifically ¬panything false
First words“By contraposition, …”“Assume for a contradiction that …”
Caution rules for writing proofs (lecture, in red)
  • Direct proof of ∀x (P(x) → Q(x)): start with “Assume x is such that P(x) is true” and end with “so Q(x) is true.”
  • By contraposition: say so (“We prove this by contraposition”).
  • By contradiction: start “Assume for a contradiction that …” and end “Contradiction!” with an explanation.
  • Don't prove the converse by accident. Assuming the conclusion and deriving the hypothesis proves q → p, not p → q.
Warm-up · not graded

  1. What's the contrapositive of “if n² is odd then n is odd”?

    Answer
    If n is even, then n² is even.

  2. Prove by contradiction that there's no largest integer.

    Hint
    Suppose N is the largest. Look at N + 1.
    Answer
    N + 1 is an integer bigger than N. Contradiction!

  3. Proving p → q by contradiction: what's your first line?

    Answer
    “Assume for a contradiction that p is true and q is false.”

Lock it down

How to do it: proof by contraposition
  1. Find p and q in “if p, then q.”
  2. Negate both, in plain words. “not even” = odd, “not irrational” = rational, “not (A and B)” = “not A or not B” (De Morgan).
  3. Write the contrapositive ¬q → ¬p. The negated conclusion goes first.
  4. First line: “By contraposition, we prove: if ¬q, then ¬p.” (Write out the actual words.)
  5. Assume ¬q and unpack it with a definition.
  6. Work forward with algebra until you reach ¬p exactly.
  7. Last line: “So ¬p. This proves the contrapositive, so p → q is true.”
  8. Check: did you start at the negated conclusion and end at the negated hypothesis? If you started at p or ended at q, it is a different proof.
How to do it: proof by contradiction
  1. Write what you want to prove. If it is “if p, then q,” its negation is p ∧ ¬q.
  2. First line: “Assume for a contradiction that p and not q.” For a plain statement like “√3 is irrational,” assume its opposite: “√3 is rational.”
  3. Unpack everything you assumed with definitions.
  4. Hunt for a clash: a number that is both even and odd, a fraction in lowest terms whose top and bottom are both even, a rational that is also irrational, an integer strictly between 0 and 1.
  5. Last line: “Contradiction! [Say exactly which two facts clash.] So the original statement is true.”
How to do it: pick the method
  1. Try direct first. Does the hypothesis unpack into a formula (n = 2k + 1, r = p/q) that you can push forward? Then go direct.
  2. Hypothesis is useless, but ¬(conclusion) is easy? Use contraposition. Signs: hypothesis is about n², 3n + 2, or 7n + 4, and the conclusion is about n.
  3. Conclusion says “irrational,” “not,” “no,” or “infinitely many”? Its negation is a positive fact you can unpack (“rational,” “there is one,” “finitely many”). Use contradiction.
  4. Not an “if…then” at all (like “√2 is irrational”)? Contraposition doesn't apply. Use contradiction.
How to do it: √2 is irrational, from memory
  1. Assume for a contradiction that √2 is rational.
  2. Write √2 = p/q, integers, q ≠ 0, in lowest terms, so p and q are not both even.
  3. Square and clear the fraction: p² = 2q². So p² is even.
  4. So p is even (by “n² even → n even”). Write p = 2k.
  5. Plug in: 4k² = 2q², so q² = 2k². So q² is even, so q is even.
  6. Contradiction! p and q are both even, but the fraction was in lowest terms.

Memory hook: lowest terms, square, p even, plug in, q even, clash.

Example contraposition with a fraction

Thm. For every nonzero real x, if x is irrational, then 1/x is irrational.

By contraposition, we prove: if 1/x is rational, then x is rational.¬q → ¬p, said in the first line Assume 1/x is rational, so 1/x = p/q for integers p, q with q ≠ 0.unpack ¬q with the definition p ≠ 01/x is never 0, so its top isn't 0 x = q/pflip both sides q, p are integers and p ≠ 0.checks the definition So x is rational. □reached ¬p exactly

Why not direct? “x is irrational” only tells you what x is not. There is no formula to plug in. The negated conclusion “1/x = p/q” gives you a formula.

Example contradiction: rational + irrational

Thm. If r is rational and x is irrational, then r + x is irrational.

Assume for a contradiction that r is rational, x is irrational, and r + x is rational.p ∧ ¬q r = c/d and r + x = a/b, integers, b ≠ 0, d ≠ 0.unpack both rationals, fresh letters x = (r + x) − r = a/b − c/dsolve for x = (ad − bc)/(bd)common denominator ad − bc and bd are integers, and bd ≠ 0.b ≠ 0 and d ≠ 0 So x is rational.definition of rational Contradiction! x is both rational and irrational. □name the clash

Use it: √2 is irrational, so 3 + √2 and −5 + √2 are irrational right away.

Common mistakes
  • Wrong: contrapositive of “if p then q” is “if ¬p then ¬q.” Right: it is “if ¬q then ¬p.” (“If ¬p then ¬q” is the inverse, and it is not equivalent. Flip and negate.)
  • Wrong: for a contradiction proof of p → q, assuming “p → ¬q” or “¬p.” Right: assume p and ¬q. (¬(p → q) ≡ p ∧ ¬q. You keep the hypothesis and deny the conclusion.)
  • Wrong: negating “a ≠ 0 and b ≠ 0” as “a = 0 and b = 0.” Right: “a = 0 or b = 0.” (De Morgan flips ∧ to ∨.)
  • Wrong: in the √2 proof, leaving out “lowest terms.” Right: say it on the line where you write √2 = p/q. (Without it, “p and q are both even” is not a contradiction at all. 2/2 is a fine fraction.)
  • Wrong: no opening phrase, so the grader can't tell which method you are using. Right: “By contraposition, …” or “Assume for a contradiction that …”. (Lecture says this is required.)

Textbook practice Rosen §1.7: 9, 13, 17, 19, 27 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

§8Cases, existence, uniqueness, counterexamples

Lecture 4 · Rosen §1.8 · HW 2.6–2.9

Split into cases that cover everything. To show something exists, find it (or show it must exist). One counterexample kills a “for all.”

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Prove by cases (parity, sign, remainders)
  • Give constructive and nonconstructive existence proofs
  • Disprove with a counterexample
  • Prove uniqueness
Memorize
  • |x| definition and the triangle inequality
  • Uniqueness = existence + (P(x) ∧ P(y) → x = y)

Proof by cases

Defn

To prove (p₁ ∨ p₂ ∨ … ∨ pₙ) → q, use [(p₁ ∨ … ∨ pₙ) → q] ≡ [(p₁ → q) ∧ … ∧ (pₙ → q)]: show each case separately implies q.

Often the hypothesis isn't an “or” yet. Then first split it into cases that cover every possibility (n even / n odd; x ≥ 0 / x < 0; n mod 3 = 0, 1, 2), and handle each.

Example Lecture 4

Thm. If n is even or m is even, then nm is even.

Case 1: n is even, so n = 2k and nm = 2(km), even. Case 2: m is even, so m = 2k and nm = 2(nk), even.

Thm. For every integer n, n(n + 1) is even.

By cases on n's parity. Case 1: n = 2k. Then n(n + 1) = 2k(2k + 1) = 2(2k² + k), even. Case 2: n = 2k + 1. Then n(n + 1) = (2k + 1)(2k + 2) = 4k² + 6k + 2 = 2(2k² + 3k + 1), even.

Thm (Lecture 9). n² + n + 1 is odd for every integer n.

Case n = 2k: n² + n + 1 = 4k² + 2k + 1 = 2(2k² + k) + 1. Case n = 2k + 1: 4k² + 4k + 1 + 2k + 1 + 1 = 4k² + 6k + 3 = 2(2k² + 3k + 1) + 1. Odd either way.

Defn

The absolute value of a real number x is |x| = x if x ≥ 0, and |x| = −x if x < 0.

Thm Lecture 4

For all real x: −|x| ≤ x ≤ |x|.

Case 1: x ≥ 0. Then |x| = x, so x ≤ |x|, and −|x| = −x ≤ 0 ≤ x. Case 2: x < 0. Then |x| = −x > 0, so x < 0 < |x|, and −|x| = x.

Triangle inequality. For all real x, y: |x + y| ≤ |x| + |y|.

Case 1: x + y ≥ 0. Then |x + y| = x + y ≤ |x| + |y|, since x ≤ |x| and y ≤ |y|. Case 2: x + y < 0. Then |x + y| = −x − y. By the previous theorem −x ≤ |x| and −y ≤ |y|, so |x + y| ≤ |x| + |y|.

Example cases mod 3 (Lecture 9)

Thm. If 3 | ab, then 3 | a or 3 | b.

By contraposition: assume 3 ∤ a and 3 ∤ b, i.e. a, b ≢ 0 (mod 3). Four cases: (a, b) ≡ (1, 1) gives ab ≡ 1; (1, 2) gives 2; (2, 1) gives 2; (2, 2) gives 4 ≡ 1 (mod 3). In every case ab ≢ 0 (mod 3), so 3 ∤ ab.

Existence proofs

Defn

To prove ∃x P(x): a constructive proof exhibits a specific x and checks P(x). A nonconstructive proof shows some x must exist without saying which one (often by contradiction or by cases).

Example constructive

There are irrational x, y with x + y rational: x = √2, y = −√2, so x + y = 0. Lecture 9: the same pair makes both x + y = 0 and xy = −2 rational.

There's an irrational y and a rational x with xʸ rational: x = 1, y = √2 gives 1^√2 = 1.

Mersenne claimed in 1644 that 2⁶⁷ − 1 is prime. In 1903 Frank Nelson Cole silently wrote 2⁶⁷ − 1 = 147,573,952,589,676,412,927 = 193,707,721 × 761,838,257,287 on the board, multiplied it out, and sat down to a standing ovation. A constructive disproof by one example.

Thm nonconstructive (Lecture 2 sidebar)

There exist irrational numbers x, y such that xʸ is rational.

Case 1: √2^√2 is rational. Take x = y = √2. Case 2: √2^√2 is irrational. Take x = √2^√2 and y = √2. Then xʸ = (√2^√2)^√2 = √2^(√2·√2) = (√2)² = 2, rational.

We never learn which case holds; that's what makes it nonconstructive. (In fact √2^√2 is irrational, by the Gelfond–Schneider theorem.)

Counterexamples

Defn

If ∀x P(x) is false, there must be some x with ¬P(x): a counterexample. One counterexample disproves a “for all.”

“If x, y are irrational then x + y is irrational”? False: counterexample x = √2, y = −√2.

When asked to disprove, give the specific counterexample and verify it satisfies the hypotheses but not the conclusion.

Sidebar the Gömböc

Every 2-D convex shape has at least two stable and two unstable equilibrium points. Mathematicians expected the same in 3-D, but in 2006 Gábor Domokos and Péter Várkonyi built a counterexample, the Gömböc: a convex solid of uniform density with exactly one stable and one unstable equilibrium. It looks a lot like an Indian star tortoise shell, since tortoises benefit from being able to right themselves.

Uniqueness proofs

Defn

To prove “there is a unique x with P(x)”, do two steps:

  1. Existence: show ∃x P(x).
  2. Uniqueness: show that if P(x) and P(y), then x = y.
Example Lecture 4

Thm. If n, m are integers and n ≠ 0, there is a unique rational r with nr + m = 0.

Existence: let r = −m/n (rational since n ≠ 0). Then nr + m = −m + m = 0. Uniqueness: suppose nr + m = 0 and ns + m = 0. Then nr = −m = ns, so r = s since n ≠ 0.

Rosen §1.8 also uses “without loss of generality” (WLOG): when two cases are symmetric (e.g. x ≤ y vs y ≤ x), prove one and say the other is the same with the roles swapped.

Warm-up · not graded

  1. Prove n² + n is even for every integer n.

    Hint
    Cases: n even, n odd. (Or notice n² + n = n(n + 1).)
    Answer
    n = 2k: 4k² + 2k = 2(2k² + k). n = 2k + 1: 4k² + 6k + 2 = 2(2k² + 3k + 1).

  2. Disprove: “every prime is odd.”

    Answer
    2 is prime and even. One counterexample is enough.

  3. Is the √2^√2 proof constructive?

    Answer
    No. It shows x, y exist without telling you which case is true.

Lock it down

How to do it: proof by cases
  1. Pick what to split on. Even/odd stuff: parity (n = 2k, n = 2k + 1). Absolute values: sign (x ≥ 0, x < 0). Divisibility by 3: remainders (n = 3k, 3k + 1, 3k + 2).
  2. Check the cases cover everything. Every possible n must land in some case. Don't forget 0 when splitting on sign.
  3. First line: “We split into cases.” Label each one: “Case 1: n = 3k for some integer k.”
  4. Prove the goal in each case separately. Each case is a mini direct proof. You may use only that case's assumption.
  5. Symmetric cases? Say “WLOG” (without loss of generality) and do just one, explaining that the other is the same with the roles swapped.
  6. Last line: “In every case, [goal]. □”
How to do it: constructive existence
  1. Goal is ∃x P(x). Play with small or simple values (0, 1, 2, √2, −√2) until one works.
  2. Write it: “Let x = ….”
  3. Verify P(x) by plugging in, showing the arithmetic.
  4. Check every condition in P, like “positive,” “distinct,” or “irrational.”
How to do it: nonconstructive existence
  1. Use this when you can prove something exists but can't point to it.
  2. Option A, cases: “Either A or not A.” Show that each case hands you a valid example, even though you don't know which case holds.
  3. Option B, contradiction: “Assume for a contradiction that no such x exists,” and derive something false.
  4. End by saying such an x exists. You do not have to say which one.
How to do it: disprove with a counterexample
  1. Write the claim as “for all x, if P(x) then Q(x).”
  2. You need one x where P(x) is true and Q(x) is false.
  3. Try small numbers, 0, negatives, 2 (the even prime), √2, and the edges of the domain.
  4. Verify both halves: “x satisfies P because …” and “Q(x) fails because …”.
  5. Write: “So the claim is false.” One example is enough.
How to do it: prove uniqueness
  1. Existence: find an x with P(x) and verify it.
  2. Uniqueness: “Suppose P(x) and P(y).” Use both facts to show x = y.
  3. Don't assume x = y anywhere. That is what you are proving.
  4. If you are dividing or cancelling, say why it's allowed (“since n ≠ 0”).
  5. Check: try to find a second solution, like −2 for x² = 4. If one exists, the uniqueness claim is false.
Example cases on remainders mod 3

Thm. For every integer n, n² has the form 3j or 3j + 1 for some integer j. (So n² never has remainder 2 when divided by 3.)

Every integer is 3k, 3k + 1, or 3k + 2 for some integer k.division by 3: remainder 0, 1, or 2 Case 1: n = 3k. n² = 9k² = 3(3k²).form 3j with j = 3k² Case 2: n = 3k + 1. n² = 9k² + 6k + 1 = 3(3k² + 2k) + 1.form 3j + 1 with j = 3k² + 2k Case 3: n = 3k + 2. n² = 9k² + 12k + 4 = 3(3k² + 4k + 1) + 1.pull 3 out of the 4: 4 = 3 + 1 In every case n² is 3j or 3j + 1. □the cases covered every n

Spot check: 5 = 3(1) + 2, and 5² = 25 = 3(8) + 1. ✓

Example cases on sign, with WLOG

Thm. For all real x, y: |xy| = |x| |y|.

Split on the signs of x and y.|·| is defined by sign Case 1: x ≥ 0, y ≥ 0. Then xy ≥ 0, so |xy| = xy = |x| |y|.|x| = x, |y| = y Case 2: x ≥ 0, y < 0. Then xy ≤ 0, so |xy| = −xy = x(−y) = |x| |y|.|z| = −z also works when z = 0 Case 3: x < 0, y ≥ 0. Same as Case 2 with x and y swapped.WLOG: symmetric case Case 4: x < 0, y < 0. Then xy > 0, so |xy| = xy = (−x)(−y) = |x| |y|.negative × negative is positive In every case |xy| = |x| |y|. □four cases cover all signs
Common mistakes
  • Wrong: cases “x > 0” and “x < 0.” Right: “x ≥ 0” and “x < 0.” (x = 0 fell through the cracks. Cases must cover everything.)
  • Wrong: “It works for n = 1, 2, 3, 4, so it's true for all n.” Right: examples prove “there exists,” and disprove “for all.” They never prove “for all.” (n² − n + 11 is prime for n = 1 to 10, then fails at n = 11.)
  • Wrong: a counterexample that breaks the hypothesis. Disproving “if x, y are irrational, then xy is irrational” with x = 2, y = 3. Right: x = y = √2: both irrational, and xy = 2 is rational. (The example must satisfy P and fail Q.)
  • Wrong: proving uniqueness by just showing one solution works. Right: also show that any two solutions are equal. (“There is a solution” and “there is only one” are different claims.)
  • Wrong: |x + y| = |x| + |y|. Right: |x + y| ≤ |x| + |y|. (x = 1, y = −1 gives 0 on the left and 2 on the right.)

Textbook practice Rosen §1.8: 3, 5, 9, 15, 19, 37 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

§9Games & strategy stealing

Lecture 4 · HW 2.10–2.11 · (no game problems on Midterm 1)

In finite two-player games with no draws, someone has a winning strategy. In Chomp, player 1 wins by “stealing” player 2's strategy.

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Explain Zermelo's theorem and the strategy-stealing idea in Chomp
Memorize
  • Finite game with no draws ⇒ someone has a winning strategy
Thm Zermelo's theorem on finite games

Suppose two players alternate moves m₁, n₁, m₂, n₂, …, m_k, n_k, each sees all earlier moves, and after k rounds exactly one player is declared the winner (no draws). Then either player I or player II has a winning strategy: a rule that says how to move on each turn, depending on the opponent's moves, that guarantees a win.

Examples: Armageddon chess (draws count as wins for Black), Go with a half-point komi.

Picture it why it's a logic fact

Let W(m₁, n₁, …, m_k, n_k) mean “player I wins.” Player I has a winning strategy iff

∃m₁ ∀n₁ ∃m₂ ∀n₂ … ∃m_k ∀n_k W(…)

Negate it: ∀m₁ ∃n₁ … ∀m_k ∃n_k ¬W(…), which says exactly that player II has a winning strategy. A statement or its negation is true, so one of them has a winning strategy. Quantifiers really are a game: ∃ is your move, ∀ is your opponent's.

Thm Chomp

Chomp: a grid of cookies whose top-left cookie is poisoned. On each turn a player picks a remaining cookie and eats it along with every cookie below and/or to the right of it. Whoever eats the poisoned cookie loses.

The first player has a winning strategy on every n × m board except 1 × 1.

By contradiction. Suppose player I has no winning strategy. By Zermelo, player II has one. Player I imagines opening by eating only the bottom-right cookie, and looks up the bite X that II's winning strategy answers with. Every bite eats the bottom-right cookie too, so the board after “bottom-right, then X” is the same as the board after X alone. So player I really opens with X, and from then on follows II's strategy. Player I is now sitting in II's winning seat, so player I has a winning strategy. Contradiction: both players can't have winning strategies.

This strategy-stealing proof is nonconstructive. It's an open problem to describe the winning strategy explicitly for all board sizes (there was progress on 3 × n boards in 2026).

Warm-up · not graded

  1. Chomp on a 1×2 board (the poisoned cookie plus one more). Who wins?

    Answer
    Player 1: eat the safe cookie, and player 2 is left with the poison.

  2. Why is the strategy-stealing argument nonconstructive?

    Answer
    It proves player 1 has a winning strategy without saying what it is.

Lock it down

How to do it: check if Zermelo's theorem applies
  1. Two players taking turns? Not simultaneous moves like rock-paper-scissors.
  2. Everyone sees every earlier move? No hidden cards, no secret moves.
  3. Finite? The game must end within some fixed number of moves. (Chomp: every bite eats at least one cookie, so an n × m board ends within nm moves.)
  4. No draws? Every finished game has exactly one winner.
  5. All four yes? Then exactly one player has a winning strategy. Zermelo doesn't tell you which player or what the strategy is.
How to do it: write and negate the quantifier statement
  1. Player I's moves get ∃ (I gets to choose). Player II's moves get ∀ (I must survive every reply).
  2. Write them in order: ∃m₁ ∀n₁ ∃m₂ ∀n₂ … W, where W means “I wins.”
  3. Read it aloud: “I has a first move such that for every reply, I has a second move such that … I wins.”
  4. To negate: flip every ∃ to ∀ and every ∀ to ∃, keep the order, and change W to ¬W.
  5. Read the result: ∀m₁ ∃n₁ … ¬W = “whatever I does, II has an answer, and II wins.” That is II's winning strategy.
How to do it: the strategy-stealing proof (Chomp)
  1. Set up: “By Zermelo, someone has a winning strategy. Assume for a contradiction that it is player II.”
  2. Find a harmless move: eating only the bottom-right cookie. Every later bite also removes that cookie, so this move can be “absorbed” into any other bite.
  3. Imagine I opens with that move. II's winning strategy answers with some bite X.
  4. Steal: the board after “bottom-right, then X” is the same as after X alone. So I simply opens with X.
  5. Swap seats: now II faces the exact board II would have handed to I. I copies II's winning strategy from there on and wins.
  6. Contradiction! Both players would have winning strategies. So player I has one. □
How to do it: find the winner of a small game by working backward
  1. Start from the end positions and label each one W (player to move wins) or L (player to move loses).
  2. A position is W if some move leads to an L position. (∃ a good move.)
  3. A position is L if every move leads to a W position. (∀ moves are bad.)
  4. Work back to the starting position. W means player I wins. L means player II wins.
  5. The winning strategy: always move to an L position.

This is exactly why Zermelo's theorem is true: every position gets a W or L label, so someone is guaranteed a win.

Example an explicit strategy on a square board

On n × n Chomp with n ≥ 2, strategy stealing says player I wins. On a square we can actually say how.

I eats the cookie diagonally below-right of the poison (row 2, column 2).this removes everything except row 1 and column 1 What's left: the poison, plus an arm of n − 1 cookies going right and an arm of n − 1 going down.an L shape with equal arms II must bite from one arm (or eat the poison and lose).no other cookies remain I bites the same number of cookies off the other arm.mirror move: arms are equal again Repeat. The arms keep shrinking.finite game, so it must end When both arms are empty, it is II's turn, and only the poison is left. II loses. □I always restores equal arms, so II faces the empty L

Brute force confirms this is the only winning first move on a 3 × 3 board. So “eat the bottom-right cookie” is a tool for the proof, not the winning move.

Example working backward

There are 7 stones. Players alternate taking 1 or 2 stones. Whoever takes the last stone wins. Who has a winning strategy?

0 stones: Lyour opponent just took the last stone 1, 2 stones: Wtake them all, landing on 0 (an L) 3 stones: Lyou leave 2 or 1, both W for your opponent 4, 5: W (leave 3). 6: Lsame pattern: multiples of 3 are L 7 stones: W, by taking 1 to leave 6a move to an L position exists After that, if II takes t, I takes 3 − t.keeps the pile a multiple of 3

Player I wins. The game is finite with no draws, so Zermelo said someone wins. Working backward told us who and how.

Common mistakes
  • Wrong: “By Zermelo, the first player wins.” Right: “By Zermelo, one of the players has a winning strategy.” (In the 9-stone version of the game above, player II wins.)
  • Wrong: using Zermelo on tic-tac-toe. Right: check for draws first. (Tic-tac-toe can end in a draw, so this version of the theorem doesn't apply.)
  • Wrong: “Strategy stealing says: open by eating the bottom-right cookie.” Right: that move is only imagined in the proof. (On 3 × 3 the bottom-right opening loses. The proof doesn't tell you the real winning move.)
  • Wrong: negating ∃m₁ ∀n₁ W as ∃m₁ ∀n₁ ¬W. Right: ∀m₁ ∃n₁ ¬W. (Every quantifier flips, not just W.)
  • Wrong: forgetting the 1 × 1 board. Right: on 1 × 1 the only cookie is poison, so player I loses. (The harmless bottom-right move doesn't exist there, so the stealing proof fails.)

Textbook practice Rosen §5.2: 15 · odd-numbered answers are in the back of the book

Part III

Sets, functions & infinity

§10Sets

Lecture 5 · Rosen §2.1 · HW 3.1–3.2, 3.4

A set is a bag of distinct items; order and repeats don't matter. Know ∈ (“is an item in”) versus ⊆ (“every item is also in”).

Goals
Walk away able to
  • Decide ∈ vs ⊆ for sets of sets
  • List power sets and Cartesian products
Memorize
  • A ⊆ B ⟺ ∀x(x ∈ A → x ∈ B)
  • A = B ⟺ A ⊆ B and B ⊆ A
  • |𝒫(A)| = 2ⁿ; ∅ ⊆ every set
  • ∅ ≠ {∅}
Defn Rosen §2.1 Def 1
