  • Base case + induction step, both labeled; state the IH
  • Strong induction assumes P(1)…P(k); base cases = how far back the step reaches
  • Well-ordering: nonempty A ⊆ ℕ has a least element
  • Fibonacci (lecture): f₀ = f₁ = 1, fₙ = fₙ₋₁ + fₙ₋₂
  • Σ i = n(n+1)/2; Σ 2ⁱ (i = 0..n) = 2ⁿ⁺¹ − 1; Σ fᵢ = fₙ₊₂ − 1
  • Hanoi: Hₙ = 2Hₙ₋₁ + 1 = 2ⁿ − 1

Counting & probability

  • Product rule; division rule n/d; |A ∪ B| = |A| + |B| − |A ∩ B|; complement
  • Pigeonhole: k + 1 in k boxes; generalized ⌈N/k⌉
  • P(n, r) = n!/(n − r)!; C(n, r) = n!/((n − r)! r!); 0! = 1
  • Repetition: nʳ (ordered), C(n + r − 1, r) (stars & bars)
  • Repeated letters: n!/(n₁!⋯nₖ!)
  • C(n, r) = C(n, n − r); Pascal; k·C(n, k) = n·C(n − 1, k − 1)
  • (x + y)ⁿ = Σ C(n, j)xⁿ⁻ʲyʲ; Σ C(n, k) = 2ⁿ
  • p(E) = |E|/|S|; p(Ē) = 1 − p(E); p(E₁∪E₂) = p(E₁) + p(E₂) − p(E₁∩E₂)

Test yourself: write this sheet from memory on blank paper, then compare. The memorize trainer's “Write from memory” mode does this and turns misses into flashcards.

Negation table

StatementNegation

p ∨ q¬p ∧ ¬q
p → qp ∧ ¬q
p ↔ qp ↔ ¬q  (≡ p ⊕ q)
∀x P(x)∃x ¬P(x)
∃x P(x)∀x ¬P(x)
∀x (P(x) → Q(x))∃x (P(x) ∧ ¬Q(x))
∃x (P(x) ∧ Q(x))∀x (P(x) → ¬Q(x))
∀x ∃y P(x, y)∃x ∀y ¬P(x, y)

Proof templates

To proveDo this
p → qDirect: “Assume p.” … “Therefore q.”
p → qContraposition: “By contraposition. Assume ¬q.” … “So ¬p.”
pContradiction: “Assume for a contradiction ¬p.” … “Contradiction!” + why
p ↔ qProve (⇒) and (⇐) separately
(p₁ ∨ … ∨ pₙ) → qCases: show each pᵢ → q
∀x P(x)“Let x be arbitrary.” Prove P(x). Never by examples.
disprove ∀x P(x)One counterexample, verified
∃x P(x)Exhibit x (constructive), or argue it must exist
∃!x P(x)Existence, then: if P(x) and P(y) then x = y
A ⊆ B“Let x ∈ A.” … “So x ∈ B.”
A = BChain of ⟺, or A ⊆ B and B ⊆ A
f injectiveAssume f(a) = f(b), show a = b
f not injectiveTwo different inputs, same output
f surjectiveLet b ∈ B; find a ∈ A with f(a) = b
f not surjectiveOne b in the codomain never hit
|A| = |B|Build a bijection (or injections both ways)
a | bWrite b = a·c with c an integer
a ≡ b (mod m)Show m | a − b
P(n) for all n ≥ bInduction: basis P(b); assume P(k), prove P(k + 1)
P(k + 1) needs several earlier casesStrong induction: assume P(b), …, P(k)
“some box must have ≥ 2”Pigeonhole: name the boxes, count the objects

Definitions to write from memory

  • even: n = 2k for some integer k; odd: n = 2k + 1 for some integer k
  • rational: r = p/q for integers p, q with q ≠ 0; irrational: real and not rational
  • |x| = x if x ≥ 0, −x if x < 0
  • A ⊆ B: ∀x (x ∈ A → x ∈ B); 𝒫(A) = {B | B ⊆ A}; A × B = {(a, b) | a ∈ A, b ∈ B}
  • injective: f(a) = f(b) → a = b; surjective: ∀b ∃a f(a) = b; bijective: both
  • |A| = |B|: bijection exists; |A| ≤ |B|: injection exists; countable: same cardinality as a subset of ℕ
  • a | b: b = ac for some integer c (a ≠ 0); a ≡ b (mod m): m | a − b
  • prime: p > 1 with only positive factors 1 and p; gcd: largest common divisor; lcm: smallest positive common multiple

Number theory toolkit

TaskTool
a mod da = dq + r with 0 ≤ r < d (remainder never negative)
base b digitsDivide by b repeatedly; remainders are the digits, last first
aⁿ mod mRepeated squaring + binary expansion of n
gcd(a, b)Euclid: gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, a mod b); last nonzero remainder
gcd, lcm from factorizationsmin / max of exponents; gcd·lcm = ab
gcd = sa + tbExtended Euclid (back-substitute)
inverse of a mod mExists iff gcd(a, m) = 1; it's s from 1 = sa + tm
ax ≡ b (mod m)d = gcd(a, m); none if d ∤ b; else x₀ = (b/d)s, all x₀ + k(m/d)
system of congruencesCRT: x = Σ aᵢMᵢyᵢ mod M (moduli pairwise coprime)
aⁿ mod prime pFermat: aᵖ⁻¹ ≡ 1 if p ∤ a, so reduce n mod p − 1
is n composite?aⁿ⁻¹ ≢ 1 (mod n) proves composite; trial division up to √n
RSAn = pq; gcd(e, (p−1)(q−1)) = 1; de ≡ 1 mod (p−1)(q−1); c = mᵉ, m = cᵈ (mod n)

step 1 and step 2multiply: n₁·n₂

N objects, k boxessome box has ≥ ⌈N/k⌉

bit strings of length n / subsets of an n-set2ⁿ

!Mistakes to avoid

Collected from every “Caution” in lecture

Logic

  • “Or” in math is inclusive. Use ⊕ for exclusive or.
  • A false hypothesis makes p → q true.
  • “p only if q” is p → q. “p unless q” is ¬q → p.
  • The converse q → p is not equivalent to p → q; the contrapositive ¬q → ¬p is.
  • “Every P is Q” uses →, not ∧. “Some P is Q” uses ∧, not →.
  • ∀x ∃y and ∃y ∀x mean different things.
  • ∀ doesn't distribute over ∨; ∃ doesn't distribute over ∧.

Proofs

  • Say “by contraposition” / “assume for a contradiction” at the start.
  • A ∀ statement is never proved by checking examples.
  • Use a different letter for each number's definition (m = 2k + 1, n = 2j + 1).
  • Don't prove the converse by accident: start from the hypothesis, not the conclusion.
  • To contradict p → q, assume p ∧ ¬q.

Sets & functions

  • ∈ vs ⊆: {1, 2} ∈ B and {1, 2} ⊆ B are different questions. ∈ isn't transitive.
  • ∅ ≠ {∅}. ∅ is a subset of everything, but an element only if listed.
  • Range ≠ codomain. Changing the domain or codomain changes injective/surjective.
  • f ∘ g applies g first.
  • f⁻¹ is the inverse function, not 1/f.

Number theory

  • a | b is true/false; b/a is a number.
  • a mod m is in {0, …, m − 1}; −11 mod 10 = 9.
  • No cancelling in congruences unless gcd(c, m) = 1.
  • 1 is not prime. p₁⋯pₙ + 1 need not be prime.
  • p | ab ⇒ p | a or p | b only works for prime p.
  • CRT needs pairwise relatively prime moduli.
  • Fermat: reduce exponents mod p − 1, and only when p is prime and p ∤ a.
  • Passing the Fermat test doesn't prove primality (pseudoprimes, Carmichael numbers).

Induction & counting

  • Assume P(k) and prove P(k + 1); never assume P(k + 1).
  • Actually use the inductive hypothesis, and don't skip the basis.
  • If the step reaches back more than one case, you need more basis cases (postage: 4).
  • Sum rule only for non-overlapping options; otherwise subtract the overlap.
  • Decide whether order matters before choosing P or C.
  • “At least one” is almost always easier as total − none.
  • Lecture Fibonacci is f₀ = f₁ = 1 (Rosen uses f₀ = 0).
  • 0! = 1, so C(n, 0) = C(n, n) = 1.
  • Stars and bars needs identical items; people are never identical.
  • Probability = |E|/|S| only for equally likely outcomes: count ordered dice pairs, not sums.
  • Label every induction proof: base case, induction step, induction hypothesis.

6.33ⁿ < n! for n ≥ 7Base n = 7: 2187 < 50403ᵏ⁺¹ = 3·3ᵏ < 3·k! < (k + 1)·k!
6.4Find the error in “max(x, y) = n ⇒ x = y”The IH needs positive integersx = 1, y = 2: x − 1 = 0 isn't positive, so the IH can't be applied (the step from 1 to 2 fails)
6.5Every n ≥ 18 is 4a + 7b (strong induction)The step reaches back 4, so 4 base cases18 = 4 + 14, 19 = 12 + 7, 20 = 4·5, 21 = 7·3; k + 1 = (k − 3) + 4
6.6a₀ = 2, aₙ₊₁ = 3aₙ + 2: compute, guess, prove2, 8, 26, 80, 242aₙ = 3ⁿ⁺¹ − 1; step 3(3ᵏ⁺¹ − 1) + 2 = 3ᵏ⁺² − 1

6.8Every positive integer is a sum of distinct Fibonacci numbersTake the largest fₙ ≤ k + 1, then use the IH on the restThe rest is < fₙ₋₁, so its sum doesn't reuse fₙ
6.9Tower of Hanoi: Hₙ = 2ⁿ − 1, and no solution does betterLook at the first and last moves of the biggest disk≥ (2ⁿ⁻¹ − 1) + 1 + (2ⁿ⁻¹ − 1) = 2ⁿ − 1
6.10p | a₁⋯aₙ ⇒ p | some aᵢInduction on n using p | ab ⇒ p | a or p | bWrite a₁⋯aₙ₊₁ = (a₁⋯aₙ)·aₙ₊₁
6.11Uniqueness of prime factorization (strong induction on N)p₁ divides q₁⋯qₛ, so p₁ = some qⱼ; cancel itN/p₁ < N has a unique factorization by the IH
6.12Every n ≥ 2 has a prime divisor (well-ordering)Take the least divisor d > 1If d = ab with 1 < a < d, then a is a smaller divisor of n; contradiction
